=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
There is a dungeon with n x m rooms arranged as a grid.
You are given a 2D array moveTime of size n x m, where moveTime[i][j] represents the minimum time in seconds when you can start moving to that room. You start from the room (0, 0) at time t = 0 and can move to an adjacent room. Moving between adjacent rooms takes exactly one second.
Return the minimum time to reach the room (n - 1, m - 1).
Two rooms are adjacent if they share a common wall, either horizontally or vertically.
 
Example 1:

Input: moveTime = [[0,4],[4,4]]
Output: 6
Explanation:
The minimum time required is 6 seconds.

At time t == 4, move from room (0, 0) to room (1, 0) in one second.
At time t == 5, move from room (1, 0) to room (1, 1) in one second.


Example 2:

Input: moveTime = [[0,0,0],[0,0,0]]
Output: 3
Explanation:
The minimum time required is 3 seconds.

At time t == 0, move from room (0, 0) to room (1, 0) in one second.
At time t == 1, move from room (1, 0) to room (1, 1) in one second.
At time t == 2, move from room (1, 1) to room (1, 2) in one second.


Example 3:

Input: moveTime = [[0,1],[1,2]]
Output: 3

 
Constraints:

2 <= n == moveTime.length <= 50
2 <= m == moveTime[i].length <= 50
0 <= moveTime[i][j] <= 10^9

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def minTimeToReach(self, moveTime: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Problem 3341 "Find Minimum Time to Reach Last Room I": There is a dungeon with n x m rooms arranged in a grid. You are given a 2D array moveTime of size n x m, where moveTime[i][j] represents the minimum time in seconds when you can start moving to that room. You start from room (0,0) at time t=0 and can move to an adjacent room. Moving between adjacent rooms takes exactly one second. Return minimum time to reach room (n-1,m-1)." I found memory of accepted solution:
```
class Solution:
    def minTimeToReach(self, moveTime: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        n, m = len(moveTime), len(moveTime[0])
        dist = [[inf]*m for _ in range(n)]
        dist[0][0] = 0
        pq = [(0,0,0)]
        while pq:
            t,i,j = heappop(pq)
            if i == n-1 and j == m-1: return t
            for di,dj in ...
                ni,nj = i+di, j+dj
                nt = max(t, moveTime[ni][nj]) + 1
                if nt < dist[ni][nj]:
                    dist[ni][nj] = nt
                    heappush(pq,(nt,ni,nj))
```
Yes.

The "cost" from u to v is `max(t, M_v)+1 - t`, which is >=1 if t>=M_v, or M_v+1-t if t<M_v, positive. So each move increases time by at least 1. Good.

Now, final code:
```
from typing import List
import heapq

class Solution:
    def minTimeToReach(self, moveTime: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        n = len(moveTime)
        m = len(moveTime[0])
        INF = 10**30
        dist = [[INF] * m for _ in range(n)]
        dist[0][0] = 0
        pq = [(0, 0, 0)]
        dirs = [(1,0),(-1,0),(0,1),(0,-1)]
        while pq:
            t, r, c = heapq.heappop(pq)
            if t != dist[r][c]:
                continue
            if r == n - 1 and c == m - 1:
                return t
            for dr, dc in dirs:
                nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m:
                    nt = max(t, moveTime[nr][nc]) + 1
                    if nt < dist[nr][nc]:
                        dist[nr][nc] = nt
                        heapq.heappush(pq, (nt, nr, nc))
        return dist[n-1][m-1]
```
Need maybe if target is start? constraints n,m>=2, no. But if return after loop, okay.

Potential issue: `t != dist[r][c]` with ints okay. Could use `if t > dist[r][c]`.

Now, let's consider if there is a more subtle issue: Dijkstra's priority queue uses time at cell. But because transition cost depends on destination moveTime, the graph is time-dependent. Is the "arrival time at v" always a function of departure time at u that is nondecreasing and satisfies FIFO. Yes. But Dijkstra's correctness for time-dependent networks with FIFO and nonnegative travel times holds. Need no proof in final.

Let's think if there could be a path where arriving at a cell later allows you to leave earlier to a neighbor because moveTime of neighbor is in past? No, leaving earlier always allows starting to move to neighbor no later; if moveTime neighbor is future, earlier departure means you wait longer at current cell, but arrival at neighbor = max(t, M)+1. class Solution:
    def minTimeToReach(self, moveTime: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        n, m = len(moveTime), len(moveTime[0])
        dist = [[inf]*m for _ in range(n)]
        dist[0][0] = 0
        pq = [(0,0,0)]
        while pq:
            t, r, c = heappop(pq)
            if t != dist[r][c]: continue
            for dr, dc in dirs:
                nr, nc = r+dr, c+dc
                if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m:
                    nt = max(t, moveTime[nr][nc]) + 1
                    if nt < dist[nr][nc]:
                        dist[nr][nc] = nt
                        heappush(pq, (nt, nr, nc))
        return dist[-1][-1]
```

Let's ensure code indentation and imports:
```
from typing import List
import heapq

class Solution:
    def minTimeToReach(self, moveTime: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        n = len(moveTime)
        m = len(moveTime[0])
        INF = 10**30
        dist = [[INF] * m for _ in range(n)]
        dist[0][0] = 0
        pq = [(0, 0, 0)]
        directions = [(1, 0), (-1, 0), (0, 1), (0, -1)]
        
        while pq:
            t, r, c = heapq.heappop(pq)
            if t != dist[r][c]:
                continue
            if r == n - 1 and c == m - 1:
                return t
            for dr, dc in directions:
                nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m:
                    nt = max(t, moveTime[nr][nc]) + 1
                    if nt < dist[nr][nc]:
                        dist[nr][nc] = nt
                        heapq.heappush(pq, (nt, nr, nc))
        
        return dist[n - 1][m - 1]
```
Should we use `if t > dist[r][c]` instead of `!=`? If dist can be updated to same value? We only update if nt < dist, so no duplicate same dist. `!=` okay. Use `>` safer if floating? ints. Use `if t != dist[r][c]`. Fine.

Now, let's think if there is any issue with `dist[0][0] = 0` and if `moveTime[0][0]` is high and there is a path that returns to start with a later time but maybe needed because from start you can leave to a neighbor with moveTime high? But you can wait at start from time 0; no need to return. If you leave start to neighbor and come back, you arrive at start later, which is worse than being at start at time 0. Since waiting at start until any departure time is possible. So no.

If you want to move from neighbor to start later, you must start moving to start at time >= moveTime[0][0]. Our transition uses moveTime[start] when neighbor->start. Good.

Now, let's maybe consider if there is a path that starts at (0,0), moves to neighbor, then back to (0,0) after moveTime[0][0] to "enter" start and then leave? But you were already at start at time 0, so no need to enter. If there were a rule that you can only leave a room after you have "started moving to it" at or after its moveTime, then initial start would be problematic. But statement says can move at t=0. So no.

Now, let's think about if there is a "minimum time to reach room (n-1,m-1)" and if target's moveTime is 0, output could be path length if all moveTimes 0. Example2 output 3 for 2x3, path length 3. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is a possible path with length > n*m due to waiting? Dijkstra can revisit? It doesn't need to revisit because earlier dominates. If longer path arrives after M, arrival later. Thus no need to take longer path to "kill time"; waiting is equivalent or better. This is important: Dijkstra with earliest arrival and implicit waiting captures all optimal strategies. Good.

If you arrive at v at t=0 but v's moveTime? MoveTime of v doesn't affect leaving. So no.

But before final, let's maybe consider if there is an even more subtle correctness issue: Dijkstra with time-dependent edge costs where the cost function is `max(t, M_v)+1` is not just FIFO but also "non-passing" and "left-continuous"? It is continuous? At t=M, max=M, arrival=M+1. For t<M, arrival=M+1 constant; for t>=M, arrival=t+1. Nondecreasing. Good.

Let's prove Dijkstra with a counterexample search:
We need find if earliest arrival labels can violate optimal substructure because a later arrival at u could lead to earlier arrival at v? FIFO says no. Let's verify with our function: arrival_v(t) = max(t, M_v)+1. If t1 < t2:
- If both < M: arrival = M+1 equal.
- If t1 < M <= t2: arrival1 = M+1, arrival2 = t2+1 <= M+1? Wait if t2 = M, arrival2 = M+1 equal. If t2 > M, arrival2 = t2+1 > M+1. So arrival1 <= arrival2.
- If both >= M: arrival1 = t1+1 < t2+1.
So yes.

But FIFO says no catching; earlier arrival at predecessor gives arrival at successor no later. So earliest arrival at each node is sufficient. Let's prove more formally:
Let A_v(t) be earliest arrival at v if you arrive at predecessor u at time t and then take edge u->v (possibly waiting). A_v(t) = max(t, M_v)+1. Nondecreasing. For any path P from source to v, arrival time is composition of nondecreasing functions, so if you arrive at each intermediate node earlier, final arrival no later. Therefore, among all paths that reach a node u, the one with earliest arrival at u dominates all others for any continuation. Thus single earliest label per node suffices. Dijkstra's relaxation from a node when popped with earliest time is enough. Good.

Before final, let's maybe consider if there is any issue with using `heapq` and `List` imports. The final code should be exactly:
```
from typing import List
import heapq

class Solution:
    def minTimeToReach(self, moveTime: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        n = len(moveTime)
        m = len(moveTime[0])
        INF = 10**30
        dist = [[INF] * m for _ in range(n)]
        dist[0][0] = 0
        pq = [(0, 0, 0)]
        dirs = [(1,0),(-1,0),(0,1),(0,-1)]
        
        while pq:
            t, r, c = heapq.heappop(pq)
            if t != dist[r][c]:
                continue
            if r == n - 1 and c == m - 1:
                return t
            for dr, dc in dirs:
                nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m:
                    nt = max(t, moveTime[nr][nc]) + 1
                    if nt < dist[nr][nc]:
                        dist[nr][nc] = nt
                        heapq.heappush(pq, (nt, nr, nc))
        
        return dist[n - 1][m - 1]
```
This is final. But let's think if `moveTime[nr][nc]` should be `moveTime[nr][nc]` or `moveTime[r][c]`? We confirmed destination. Good.

Let's maybe consider if there is a possible "minimum time when you can start moving to that room" and if you are in current room, to move to neighbor, you need to wait until `moveTime[neighbor]`; yes.

Now, final answer.</think>```python
from typing import List
import heapq

class Solution:
    def minTimeToReach(self, moveTime: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        n = len(moveTime)
        m = len(moveTime[0])
        INF = 10**30

        dist = [[INF] * m for _ in range(n)]
        dist[0][0] = 0

        pq = [(0, 0, 0)]
        directions = [(1, 0), (-1, 0), (0, 1), (0, -1)]

        while pq:
            t, r, c = heapq.heappop(pq)

            if t != dist[r][c]:
                continue

            if r == n - 1 and c == m - 1:
                return t

            for dr, dc in directions:
                nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc

                if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m:
                    nt = max(t, moveTime[nr][nc]) + 1

                    if nt < dist[nr][nc]:
                        dist[nr][nc] = nt
                        heapq.heappush(pq, (nt, nr, nc))

        return dist[n - 1][m - 1]
```
